Electroweak splitting functions in the Standard Model and beyond Preprint: FR-PHENO-2025-003
Stefan Dittmaier, and Max Reyer

Abstract
We derive quasi-collinear factorization formulas in generic spontaneously broken gauge theories with scalars, fermions, and vector bosons. Specifically, we obtain polarized leading-order splitting functions for all possible final-state and initial-state 1 → 2 1\to 2 processes in the considered gauge theory. The main complication lies in the presence of mass-singular terms in longitudinal polarization vectors, prohibiting the direct application of the usual factorization procedure known from Quantum Electrodynamics and Quantum Chromodynamics. We overcome this issue with two different strategies, using gauge invariance and Ward identities as guiding principle. Our derivations do not use any explicit component-wise parametrizations of momenta and wave functions and bear no reference to a particular Lorentz frame. Furthermore, our results are valid for completely general definitions of the spin reference axes of the individual external particles. The various massless limits, the special case of the Electroweak Standard Model, the reproduction of existing literature results, and symmetry relations among our splitting functions are discussed in detail.



1
Introduction

The 2012 discovery
ATLAS:2012yve
;
CMS:2012qbp
of the Higgs boson
and the ensuing confirmation of its properties provided the last missing
piece of experimental confirmation of the
Electroweak Standard Model (EWSM)
.
Since then, no clear signs of physics
beyond the Standard Model have
been observed
Workman:2022ynf
, despite the plethora of data taken
at the
Large Hadron Collider (LHC)
at different center-of-mass energies of up to
\sqrt{s}=13.6\,\text{TeV}
.
Such new physics is needed to explain, for example, the observation of
dark matter on cosmological scales in the framework of particle physics.
In recent years, much of the experimental effort has therefore shifted
to the determination of observables and cross sections
with increasing precision.
For a meaningful comparison with the expectations from the
EWSM
,
the uncertainties from the corresponding theoretical predictions should
ideally be lower than those from the measurements.
For many key processes
at the
LHC
, this requires computations at
next-to-next-to-leading order (NNLO)
in
Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD)
and
next-to-leading order (NLO)
in
electroweak (EW)
theory.
On top of that, resummation techniques such as
parton-shower
simulations
(see
Ellis1991qcd
, for example) have proven to be of
great importance for the proper prediction of kinematic distributions.

Parton-shower simulations account for the well-known fact that
scattering amplitudes of gauge theories become large when two external
particles are approximately
collinear
,
if the typical energy
scale of the considered process is much greater than the masses of the
involved particles.
This strongly enhances the production of additional, collimated
particles.
Specifically, parton-shower simulations re-sum the leading corrections
associated to the production of arbitrarily many additional collinear
particles.
Therein, the involved matrix elements are approximated via

quasi-collinear factorization formulas
, which express squared
matrix elements as products of underlying matrix elements
for a hard mother process and universal radiation factors.
These quasi-collinear
leading-order
(LO)
splitting
functions

Altarelli:1977zs
capture the collinear enhancements.
For massless particles, splitting functions are textbook knowledge;
for massive fermions,
they are known for

Quantum Electrodynamics (QED)

Baier:1973ms
;
Kleiss:1986ct
;
dittmaier1999photonrad
;
dittmaier2008polarized

and
QCD

Keller:1998tf
;
Catani:2000ef
;
catani2002massivedipole
.
Apart from parton-shower algorithms,
splitting functions also are essential ingredients to
subtraction
methods
for
NLO
computations
Ellis:1980wv
;
Frixione:1995ms
;
catani1996general
;
dittmaier1999photonrad
;
Phaf:2001gc
;
catani2002massivedipole
;
dittmaier2008polarized
;
Frederix:2009yq
.

For scattering energies in the TeV and multi-TeV range,
EW
corrections have been
found to be substantial, often reaching 10–30% already for a few TeV with a
tendency of logarithmic increase, which renders this effect already relevant in LHC
analyses. This observation triggered many studies of the
logarithmic structure of virtual
EW
corrections at
NLO
denner2001oneloop1
;
denner2001oneloop2
,
NNLO
Hori:2000tm
;
Beenakker:2001kf
;
Denner:2003wi
;
Jantzen:2005xi
, and
beyond
Ciafaloni:1999ub
;
Fadin:1999bq
;
Kuhn:1999nn
;
Melles:2001ye
;
Chiu:2007yn
;
Chiu:2008vv

(see, e.g.,
Denner:2019vbn
for further references).
Real-emission effects, on the other hand, were mostly left for process-specific LO studies with
multi-purpose Monte Carlo generators
(see, e.g.,
Baur:2006sn
).
A potential future hadron-collider experiment
is expected to reach center-of-mass energies of
about
100\,\text{TeV}

FCC:2018vvp
, much larger even than the masses
of
EW
bosons.
This renders
EW
multi-particle emission an important effect and calls for the extension
of parton-shower and subtraction algorithms
to
EW
theory.
At its core, this requires the derivation of quasi-collinear
factorization formulas in the full
EWSM
, which is the main focus
of this paper.
In fact, we derive splitting functions in the broader setting of generic
spontaneously broken gauge theories with scalars, fermions, and vector bosons.
This is supported by a discussion of gauge couplings, quantization,

spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB)
, and
Ward identities (WIs)
in this general context.
We pay particular attention to symmetry relations among
the derived splitting functions,
their various massless limits, and the comparison to results
existing in the literature.

First results on LO
EW
splitting functions in the

unbroken phase
of the
EWSM
have been reported
in
Hebenstreit:2006
;
Christiansen:2014kba
;
Bauer:2018xag
.
These derivations strictly assume the high-energy limit and neglect all
effects of the
EW
symmetry-breaking scale
v
.
In particular, all fields are assumed massless and usually taken in the
interaction basis.
Amplitudes for longitudinally polarized vector bosons are replaced by
amplitudes with would-be Goldstone bosons via the

Goldstone-boson equivalence theorem (GBET)

Gounaris:1986cr
;
Cornwall:1974km
;
Chanowitz:1985hj
;
Lee:1977eg
;
Vayonakis:1976vz
;
Denner:1996gb
.
While constituting an important first step, this approach is neither
capable of capturing the evolution of
EW
showers all the way down
to the
EW
scale
v
, nor of extracting all collinearly enhanced
terms needed, e.g., for the construction of
EW
subtraction methods.

More recently, LO splitting functions in the
broken
phase
of the
EWSM
have been
obtained
Chen:2016wkt
;
Cuomo:2019siu
;
Kleiss:2020rcg
;
Brooks:2021kji
;
Masouminia:2021kne
.
All of these derivations are based on the evaluation of
splitting
amplitudes
for
1\to 2
splitting processes with off-shell incoming
momentum and on-shell polarization vectors and spinors.
This relies on non-trivial, well-defined decompositions of the
numerators of off-shell propagators into on-shell polarization sums.
The consistency of this approach, however, is explicitly proven only
in
Chen:2016wkt
;
Cuomo:2019siu
.
Moreover, by nature of these derivations, the precise definition of
polarization vectors and spinors for the mother particles are assumed to
be identical to those of the daughter particles.
These are usually defined in the lab frame.
The decomposition into transverse and longitudinal polarization vectors
for massive vector bosons is, however, frame dependent.
This is problematic in view of subtraction formalisms.
1
1

1

In the dipole subtraction formalism
catani1996general
;
dittmaier1999photonrad
;
Phaf:2001gc
;
catani2002massivedipole
;
dittmaier2008polarized
, for example, the individual
dipole terms need to be integrated in auxiliary frames determined by the dipole
kinematics.
Performing the azimuthal-angle integral around the collinear axis requires
the separation of longitudinal and transverse components in the
specific frame of integration.
Because this frame differs from the lab frame, it is not clear whether
the existing splitting functions from the literature are readily
suited for the construction of dipole functions.

Additionally, for practical applications, the splitting amplitudes first
have to be combined into factorization formulas for the
squared

real-emission amplitudes.
Where this has been done explicitly in the literature, flavor and spin
interference effects have at least been partially neglected.
Finally,
Chen:2016wkt
;
Cuomo:2019siu
;
Masouminia:2021kne
rely on
explicit, component-wise, and frame-dependent parametrizations of
momenta, spinors, and polarization vectors.
Only the derivation from
Kleiss:2020rcg
;
Brooks:2021kji
is
conducted in a way that bears no explicit reference to a specific frame.

In order to overcome these issues, we aim at a factorization directly at
the level of squared matrix elements and employ
general
Sudakov parametrizations

to describe the quasi-collinear kinematics.
The latter express the splitting momenta in terms of generic
four-vectors in a Lorentz-covariant way.
This allows us to write collinear factorization formulas via
contractions of polarization-vector-stripped hard matrix elements and
tensor-valued splitting functions,
encoding potential spin
correlations.
In this approach, the decomposition of internal propagator numerators
into frame-dependent polarization vectors is circumvented.
We avoid the precise definition of polarization altogether by
not making any assumption on the spin reference axes for the individual
external particles.
This provides a further reduction of assumptions compared to the
existing literature.
Finally, working in a generic spontaneously broken gauge theory is a feature shared
only by
Cuomo:2019siu
.

The derivation of splitting functions in gauge theories with massive
vector bosons provides a serious challenge.
Specifically, in the high-energy limit, the polarization vector of a
longitudinally polarized vector boson with momentum
p^{\mu}
and mass

M_{V}
diverges like
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mu}(p)\sim p^{\mu}/M_{V}
.
Gauge invariance, however, dictates that this mass-singular factor
cancels in the computation of full amplitudes.
These gauge cancellations usually involve all kinds of diagrams for a
given process.
When deriving approximate factorization formulas, however,
only certain diagram types are considered.
In such a procedure, great care must be taken to not destroy the
sensitive gauge cancellations.
In previous publications, this issue has been dealt with in multiple
ways.
In
Chen:2016wkt
, a customary axial gauge is chosen for which this
problem is absent.
An axial gauge has also been used in
Borel:2012by
for proving the

effective W approximation (EWA)

Dawson:1984gx
.
In
Cuomo:2019siu
,
WIs
are employed to remove the
problematic terms
\propto p^{\mu}/M_{V}
from
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mu}

and to replace them with well-behaved would-be Goldstone-boson amplitudes.
This is achieved by a redefinition of the
four-vector
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mu}
, introducing an extra fifth
component associated to the corresponding would-be Goldstone boson.
A similar strategy, although in much less generality, has first been
used by
Dawson:1984gx
for the derivation of the
EWA
.
In this specific situation, which concerns the emission of a vector
boson off a massless fermion, the contributions
\propto p^{\mu}/M_{V}

become zero.
Without modification,the authors of
Masouminia:2021kne

coin this assumption
“Dawson’s strategy” and apply it to
all
kinds of
EW
splitting
processes, despite not being generally valid.
The results of
Masouminia:2021kne
are, therefore, in conflict
both with the existing
literature
Chen:2016wkt
;
Cuomo:2019siu
;
Kleiss:2020rcg
;
Brooks:2021kji

and our findings.
Lastly, the derivation in
Kleiss:2020rcg
;
Brooks:2021kji
relies on
carefully identifying
and eliminating all ill-behaved terms that must cancel against
contributions from neglected diagrams.

In this paper, we address the mass-singular terms in longitudinal
polarization vectors with two different strategies.
Our default approach is similar to
Cuomo:2019siu
.
However, we work in the standard formalism, avoiding the redefinition of
polarization vectors.
As an alternative, we outline a procedure close
to
Kleiss:2020rcg
;
Brooks:2021kji
, where we identify and manually
cancel ill-behaved terms.
We show that the two
methods produce equivalent results,
providing a check on
the obtained splitting functions.

Collinear enhancements also occur in one-loop virtual corrections.
The corresponding leading double and single logarithmic contributions
have been derived in
denner2001oneloop1
;
denner2001oneloop2
, where
a generic notation for gauge-theory couplings is used.
We adopt and extend this notation in the following, facilitating the
combination of these results with our factorization formulas.
In particular, this is of interest for the assessment of partial
cancellations between real and virtual corrections for specific
processes and observables, as for instance observed in
Baur:2006sn
.
This is, however, left for future work.
Moreover, while we take special care that our results are suitable for
the use in subtraction algorithms, we leave this application for a
future publication as well.

This paper is structured as follows.
We start with a detailed discussion of generic
spontaneously broken gauge theories
in Section
2
.
Particular emphasis is put on
gauge-invariance relations like
WIs
and on the resulting
relations between gauge and would-be Goldstone-boson couplings.
In Section
3
, we describe our quasi-collinear
factorization procedure and detail the issues that arise with
longitudinally polarized vector bosons.
This is spelled out explicitly in Section
4
,
where we derive our
final-state
(FS) splitting functions, discuss their
properties, and compute the azimuthal averages.
We also describe symmetry relations and use them to derive

initial-state
(IS) splitting functions.
In Section
5
, we compare our methodology
and results to the existing
literature
Chen:2016wkt
;
Cuomo:2019siu
;
Kleiss:2020rcg
;
Brooks:2021kji
;
Masouminia:2021kne
,
before we conclude in Section
6
.
We close this paper with supplementary material.
First, Appendix
A
specializes the discussion from
Section
2
to the
EWSM
and provides
explicit expressions for the corresponding coupling factors.
In Appendix
B
, we derive the
coupling relations presented in Section
2.6
.
Our alternative strategy for the treatment of the problematic,
mass-singular terms in longitudinal polarization vectors is explained in
Appendix
C
.
Appendix
D
details the recovery of
QED
and

QCD
literature results from our splitting functions.
Finally, Appendix
E
provides further
details on FS splittings in fermionic processes.

2
Generic treatment of spontaneously broken gauge theories

2.1
Generic gauge couplings

We consider
Yang–Mills (YM)
theories built from matter
fields that transform as multiplets
\varphi
in unitary
representations of a gauge group
G
.
We consider fermion and scalar multiplets
\varphi=f

and
\varphi=\phi
, respectively.
Denoting by
\varphi_{k}
the
k
-th component of the multiplet,
the variation of
\varphi_{k}
under global gauge
transformations with infinitesimal group parameter
\delta\theta_{V_{a}}
reads

\displaystyle\delta\varphi_{k}(x)=\mathrm{i}eI^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}\varphi_{l}(x)\delta\theta_{V_{a}},

(1)

where
e
is the coupling strength, playing the role of the electric unit
charge in the
EWSM
.
Following the notation of
denner2001oneloop1
;
denner2001oneloop2
,
the objects
I^{V_{a}}
correspond to the
generators of the
gauge group
G
, and the matrices
I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}
to their
representation on the space of
\varphi_{l}
.
The fields
V_{a}
denote the respective gauge fields and transform under
global gauge transformations via (
1
) themselves,
with
I^{V_{a}}_{V_{b}V_{c}}
corresponding to the generators in the adjoint
representation.

The antifield of
\varphi_{k}
is denoted by
\bar{\varphi}_{k}
.
Up to manipulations in the internal space of the fields that commute
with the gauge group generators,

\bar{\varphi}_{k}
is obtained by complex
conjugation.
Specifically, we have
\bar{}\phi_{k}=\phi_{k}^{\ast}
for scalars, while

\bar{f}_{k}=f_{k}^{\dagger}\gamma_{0}
for fermions, and
\bar{V}_{a}(x)=V_{a}^{\ast}(x)
for gauge bosons.
The transformation behavior of the multiplets
\bar{\varphi}
is
therefore obtained by conjugation of (
1
).

In
YM
theories, the components
I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}}

of the generators determine the couplings between the gauge fields
V_{a}

and multiplet components of
\varphi
.
For fermion and scalar multiplets, the couplings stem from the covariant
derivative

\displaystyle D^{\mu}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}=\delta_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}\partial^{\mu}-\mathrm{i}eI^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}V_{a}^{\mu}(x),

(2)

which appear in the Lagrangian as terms

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\bar{f}_{k}(x)\not{D}_{f_{k}f_{l}}f_{l}(x)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad

\displaystyle\big(D_{\phi_{k}\phi_{l},\mu}\phi_{l}(x)\big)^{\dagger}\big(D^{\mu}_{\phi_{k}\phi_{m}}\phi_{m}(x)\big)

(3)

that are invariant under local gauge transformations.
For matter fields, the local transformations correspond to (
1
)
with parameters
\delta\theta_{V_{a}}\equiv\delta\theta_{V_{a}}(x)
depending on
the space–time point
x
, while

\displaystyle\delta V^{\mu}_{a}(x)=\mathrm{i}eI^{V_{b}}_{V_{a}V_{c}}V^{\mu}_{c}(x)\delta\theta_{V_{b}}(x)+\partial^{\mu}\delta\theta_{V_{a}}(x).

(4)

The pure gauge-field couplings originate from the field-strength tensor

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}^{V_{a}}=\partial_{\mu}V^{a}_{\nu}(x)-\partial_{\nu}V^{a}_{\mu}(x)-\mathrm{i}eI^{V_{b}}_{V_{a}V_{c}}V^{b}_{\mu}(x)V^{c}_{\nu}(x),

(5)

which appears in the Lagrangian as locally gauge-invariant term

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}F^{\bar{V}_{a}}_{\mu\nu}F^{V_{a}\mu\nu}.

(6)

The coupling factor corresponding to the
V_{a}\bar{\varphi}_{k}\varphi_{l}
vertex reads
\mathrm{i}eI^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}
, where
the fields
V_{a}
,
\bar{\varphi}_{k}
, and
\varphi_{l}
are defined as
incoming.
The coupling
\bar{V}_{a}\varphi_{k}\bar{\varphi}_{l}
is determined by

\displaystyle I^{\bar{V}_{a}}_{\bar{\varphi}_{k}\bar{\varphi}_{l}}=-\left(I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}\right)^{\ast},

(7)

which follows from conjugation of (
1
).
Unitarity of global gauge transformations implies

\displaystyle I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{\varphi}_{l}\bar{\varphi}_{k}}=-I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}

(8)

and provides a relation to the
V_{a}\varphi_{l}\bar{\varphi}_{k}
vertex.
Relations (
7
)
and (
8
) combine to

\displaystyle I^{\bar{V}_{a}}_{\varphi_{l}\varphi_{k}}=\left(I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}\right)^{\ast},

(9)

meaning that generators for real
V_{a}
, i.e. generators from the real
Lie algebra of the gauge transformation, are hermitian.
On top of the symmetries (
7
)
and (
8
), the gauge-boson coupling
matrices
I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{V}_{b}V_{c}}
fulfill the relations

\displaystyle I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{V}_{b}V_{c}}=I^{V_{b}}_{\bar{V}_{c}V_{a}}=I^{V_{c}}_{\bar{V}_{a}V_{b}}.

(10)

Couplings that do not involve gauge bosons cannot
immediately be read off from the gauge structure alone.
They depend on the specifics of the considered model, i.e. on terms of the Lagrangian that are not dictated solely by
the requirement of local gauge invariance.
In this paper, we assume
YM
theories that experience

SSB
via a potential
V(\phi)
in the
scalar sector.
Such theories contain a set of scalar would-be Goldstone bosons
G_{a}
,
which are intimately linked to the longitudinal degrees of freedom of
the massive gauge bosons
V_{a}
that correspond to broken symmetries.
While general scalar couplings of type
\phi_{m}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}

depend on the specific structure of the theory, the couplings
G_{a}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}
of would-be Goldstone bosons are related to the
corresponding gauge-boson couplings
V_{a}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}

by gauge invariance expressed in terms of
WIs
.
These coupling relations are discussed in Section
2.6
.
Expressing gauge invariance of the theory,
these relations are crucial to guarantee the
absence of unphysical behavior in physical amplitudes
\mathcal{M}
in general and
the independence of
\mathcal{M}
of gauge parameters of the gauge-fixing procedure
in particular.
In the derivation of EW splitting functions these relations lead to
a meaningful combination of Feynman diagrams related
by the interchange of would-be Goldstone bosons and gauge bosons on
both
internal and external
lines.

The specialization of the generic couplings to the case of the
EWSM
is
described in App.
A
.

2.2
Quantization and gauge fixing

Employing the standard Faddeev–Popov prodecure to define the functional
integral of the quantized gauge theory, we extend the classical
Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{class}}
to
the effective Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}[\varphi,u,\bar{u};x]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{class}}[\varphi;x]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{fix}}[V,G;x]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ghost}}[V,G,H,u,\bar{u};x],

(11)

which does not only involve the matter and gauge fields, generically denoted
by
\varphi
, but also
auxiliary ghost fields
u^{V_{a}}
and antighost fields
\bar{u}^{V_{a}}
corresponding
to the gauge field
V^{a}
.
The gauge-fixing Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{fix}}[V,G;x]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\xi_{V_{a}}}C^{\bar{V}_{a}}[V,G;x]C^{V_{a}}[V,G;x]

(12)

breaks gauge invariance by the gauge-non-invariant functionals

C^{V_{a}}
for the gauge degree of freedom associated with
V^{a}
.
Here,
\xi_{V_{a}}=\xi_{\bar{V}_{a}}
is a (real)
gauge-fixing parameter which is widely arbitrary.
For spontaneously broken symmetries,
C^{V_{a}}
also involves the corresponding
would-be Goldstone-boson field
G^{a}
.
The ghost Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ghost}}[V,G,H,u,\bar{u};x]

\displaystyle=-\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\bar{u}^{V_{a}}(x)\,\frac{\delta C^{V_{a}}[V,G;x]}{\delta\theta_{V_{b}}(y)}\,u^{V_{b}}(y),

(13)

is derived from the gauge variations of the functionals
C^{V_{a}}
,
which in general depend
on gauge fields
V
, would-be Goldstone fields
G
, and Higgs fields
H

that combine with the would-be Goldstone fields to complete gauge multiplets.

Unlike
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{class}}
, the effective Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}

can be directly used to evaluate Green functions and amplitudes via perturbation theory.
It is neither invariant under global nor local gauge transformations, but
possesses the global
Becchi–Rouet–Stora–Tyutin (BRST)
symmetry by extending gauge transformations to the
ghost sector.
For the original fields
\varphi
of the theory, the
BRST
transformations
just take the form of local gauge transformations (
1
)
and (
4
) with gauge group
parameters
\delta\theta^{V_{a}}(x)=\delta\lambda\,u^{V_{a}}(x)
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,\varphi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}e\,\delta\lambda\,I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}u^{V_{a}}(x)\varphi_{l}(x),

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,V_{a}^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}e\,\delta\lambda\,I^{V_{b}}_{V_{a}V_{c}}u^{V_{b}}(x)V_{c}^{\mu}(x)+\delta\lambda\,\partial^{\mu}u^{V_{a}}(x),

(14)

where
\delta\lambda
is an anticommuting infinitesimal parameter, so that
the
BRST
transformations leave the classical
Lagrangian invariant,
\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{class}}=0
.
The
BRST
invariance of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is guaranteed
upon defining the
BRST
transformations of the ghost and antighost fields
according to

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,u^{V_{a}}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{i}e}{2}\delta\lambda I^{V_{b}}_{V_{a}V_{c}}u^{V_{b}}(x)u^{V_{c}}(x),\qquad\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,\bar{u}^{V_{a}}(x)=-\frac{\delta\lambda}{\xi_{V_{a}}}C^{\bar{V}_{a}}\big[V,G;x\big],

(15)

from which

\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,\big(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{fix}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ghost}}\big)=0

can be seen easily.
Defining the
BRST
operator
s
as

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{BRST}}\,\varphi_{k}(x)=\delta\lambda\,(s\varphi_{k}(x)),

(16)

the ghost Lagrangian can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ghost}}\big[V,G,H,u,\bar{u};x\big]=-\bar{u}^{V_{a}}(x)sC^{V_{a}}[V,G;x].

(17)

The explicit form of
s
for the
EWSM
can be found, for example,
in
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn
.

2.3
Spontaneous symmetry breaking

We consider
YM
theories whose gauge symmetry is spontaneously
broken via a non-zero
vacuum expectation value (vev)

\langle\phi\rangle
of the
scalar fields, representing the location of a minimum of the gauge-invariant scalar
potential
V(\phi)
.
Inserting the parametrization

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(x)=\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)+\langle\phi_{i}\rangle,

(18)

of a complex scalar field
\phi
into its kinetic Lagrangian
(second term in (
3
)), the
vev
produces
a bilinear gauge-boson term

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{M_{V}}=-e^{2}\left[I^{V_{a}}_{\phi_{i}\phi_{j}}I^{\bar{V}_{b}}_{\phi_{i}^{\ast}\phi_{k}^{\ast}}\langle\phi_{j}\rangle\langle\phi_{k}^{\ast}\rangle\right]V_{a,\mu}(x)\bar{V}_{b}^{\mu}(x)

(19)

as well as a scalar–gauge-boson mixing term

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{GV}=-\mathrm{i}e\left[\left(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\prime\ast}\right)I^{V_{a}}_{\phi_{i}\phi_{j}}\langle\phi_{j}\rangle+\left(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\prime}\right)I^{V_{a}}_{\phi_{i}^{\ast}\phi_{j}^{\ast}}\langle\phi_{j}^{\ast}\rangle\right]V_{a}^{\mu}.

(20)

The
vev

\langle\phi\rangle
is invariant under the action
of a subgroup
G^{\prime}
of the gauge group
G
.
The generators
I^{V^{\prime}_{a}}

of
G^{\prime}
correspond to
unbroken
symmetries, and their
representations on the space of
\phi
fulfill

\displaystyle I^{V^{\prime}_{a}}_{\phi_{i}\phi_{j}}\langle\phi_{j}\rangle=I^{V^{\prime}_{a}}_{\phi_{i}^{\ast}\phi_{j}^{\ast}}\langle\phi_{j}^{\ast}\rangle=0,

(21)

while the generators of
broken
symmetries do not produce zero in all these
equations.
Diagonalizing the mass terms in (
19
) yields

\dim G^{\prime}
gauge fields of massless gauge bosons and

\dim G-\dim G^{\prime}
fields of massive gauge bosons.

For each of the massive fields
V_{a}
, (
20
)
describes mixing with the associated scalar would-be Goldstone-boson
field
G_{a}
,

\displaystyle G_{a}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{e\,\eta_{V_{a}}}{M_{V_{a}}}\left(I^{\bar{V}_{a}}_{\phi_{i}^{\ast}\phi_{j}^{\ast}}\langle\phi_{j}^{\ast}\rangle\phi_{i}(x)+I^{\bar{V}_{a}}_{\phi_{i}\phi_{j}}\langle\phi_{j}\rangle\phi^{\ast}_{i}(x)\right),

(22)

with
\langle G_{a}\rangle=0
and a phase
\eta_{V_{a}}
which is fixed by
some convention.
Specifically, denoting the remaining scalar fields in a mass-diagonal field
basis as
H_{m}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{GV}=-\mathrm{i}e\left(\partial_{\mu}G_{b}^{\ast}\right)I^{V_{a}}_{G_{b}H_{m}}\langle H_{m}\rangle V_{a}^{\mu},\qquad I^{V_{a}}_{G_{b}H_{m}}\langle H_{m}\rangle=\frac{\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}}{e}\delta_{V_{a}V_{b}}.

(23)

Requiring that the would-be Goldstone-boson field for
\bar{V}_{a}
is given
by
\bar{G}_{a}=G_{a}^{\ast}
yields

\displaystyle\eta_{\bar{V}_{a}}=-\eta_{V_{a}}^{\ast}.

(24)

In practice it is very convenient to eliminate
the mixing terms upon choosing so-called

R_{\xi}
gauge-fixing functionals
in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{fix}}
,

\displaystyle C^{\bar{V}_{a}}=\partial_{\mu}\bar{V}_{a}^{\mu}+\mathrm{i}\xi_{V_{a}}\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}\bar{G}_{a}.

(25)

Massless gauge-boson fields
V_{a}
do not possess would-be
Goldstone-boson fields, i.e. the second term on the r.h.s. in (
25
) vanishes.
For massive gauge bosons
V_{a}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{fix}}

introduces mass terms for the corresponding would-be Goldstone bosons
G^{a}
.
The squared masses of
V_{a}
and
G_{a}
are related by

M_{G_{a}}^{2}=\xi_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}^{2}
.

From the gauge transformations
of (
25
), the ghost Lagrangian in
R_{\xi}
gauge
is readily obtained as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ghost}}

\displaystyle=-\bar{u}^{V_{a}}\left(\partial_{\mu}D^{\mu}_{V_{a}V_{b}}-e\xi_{V_{a}}\eta_{\bar{V}_{a}}M_{V_{a}}\left[I^{V_{b}}_{G_{a}G_{c}}G_{c}+I^{V_{b}}_{G_{a}H_{m}}H_{m}\right]\right)u^{V_{b}},

(26)

which contains mass terms for the ghost fields corresponding to massive gauge bosons with

M^{2}_{u^{V_{a}}}=\xi_{V_{a}}M^{2}_{V_{a}}
.
Moreover, (
26
) yields
gauge-boson–ghost couplings proportional to gauge group generators.

Both the ghost and would-be Goldstone-boson fields are unphysical, i.e. they do not
create and annihilate particle states.
The ghost fields are mere auxiliary fields
introduced to define a proper separation of physical and unphysical (gauge) modes
of the gauge fields, and the would-be Goldstone fields deliver the necessary
degrees of freedom to open
physical longitudinal polarizations of massive gauge bosons.
The remaining scalar fields
H_{m}
are physical, i.e. Higgs fields if they
are contained in scalar multiplets together with would-be Goldstone fields.

Finally,
SSB
may account for Dirac mass terms via
Yukawa-type terms

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Yuk}}=\bar{f}^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}(x)\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}(x)\phi_{m}(x)+\bar{f}^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}(x)\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}^{\ast}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}(x)\phi_{m}^{\ast}(x)+\mathrm{h.c.},

(27)

see, for example,
Weinberg:1996kr
;
bohm2001gauge
.
2
2

2

Note that both
bohm2001gauge
and
Weinberg:1996kr
assume
a theory with a real scalar multiplet.
Furthermore, the sign in front of the second term in (21.2.13)
of
Weinberg:1996kr
should be
-1
, and (4.1.77)
in
bohm2001gauge
is also incorrect as printed.
A corrected version of the latter is available in the official
errata
bohm2001gauge-errata
.

These respect gauge invariance if

\displaystyle-I^{V_{a}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{L}}_{k}}\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{k}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}+I^{V_{a}}_{f^{\mathrm{R}}_{k}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{k}}+I^{V_{a}}_{\phi_{n}\phi_{m}}\Upsilon^{\phi_{n}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle-I^{V_{a}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{L}}_{k}}\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}^{\ast}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{k}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}+I^{V_{a}}_{f^{\mathrm{R}}_{k}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}^{\ast}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{k}}+I^{V_{a}}_{\phi_{n}^{\ast}\phi_{m}^{\ast}}\Upsilon^{\phi_{n}^{\ast}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}

\displaystyle=0.

(28)

Upon inserting (
18
), we obtain Dirac mass terms
with the mass matrix

\displaystyle M_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}=-\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}\langle\phi_{m}\rangle-\Upsilon^{\phi_{m}^{\ast}}_{f^{\mathrm{L}}_{i}f^{\mathrm{R}}_{j}}\langle\phi_{m}^{\ast}\rangle,

(29)

which can be diagonalized with real, non-negative eigenvalues via a
bi-unitary field transformation.

2.4
Feynman rules

Following the preceding discussion, we obtain generic Feynman rules
for a spontaneously broken gauge theory with
scalars, fermions, and vector bosons, in
R_{\xi}
gauge.
We adapt them from
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn
.

S
, fermions
f
, antifermions
\bar{f}
, and vector
bosons
V
with on-shell momentum
p

(30)

(32)

The propagators read

\displaystyle G^{f\bar{f}}(-p,p)={}

f
\bar{f}
p

\displaystyle={}\dfrac{\mathrm{i}(\not{p}+m_{f})}{p^{2}-m_{f}^{2}},

\displaystyle G^{S\bar{S}}(-p,p)={}

S
\bar{S}
p

\displaystyle={}\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{p^{2}-m_{S}^{2}},

\displaystyle G^{V_{\mu_{1}}\bar{V}_{\mu_{2}}}(-p,p)={}

V_{\mu_{1}}
\bar{V}_{\mu_{2}}
p

\displaystyle={}\dfrac{-\mathrm{i}g_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}{p^{2}-M_{V}^{2}}+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}(1-\xi_{V})p_{\mu_{1}}p_{\mu_{2}}}{(p^{2}-M_{V}^{2})(p^{2}-\xi_{V}M_{V}^{2})},

(33)

where the mass of a would-be Goldstone boson
S=G_{a}
depends
on
\xi_{V_{a}}
and reads
M_{G_{a}}=\sqrt{\xi_{V_{a}}}M_{V_{a}}
.

In the following, we list the 3-particle vertices.
All fields and momenta are defined as incoming.
Momentum assignments are explicitly provided only where the
momenta appear in
the kinematic structure of the vertices.
We have

V_{a,\mu_{1}}
V_{b,\mu_{2}}
V_{c,\mu_{3}}
p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}eI^{V_{a}}_{\bar{V}_{b}V_{c}}[g_{{\mu_{1}}{\mu_{2}}}(p_{1}-p_{2})_{\mu_{3}}+g_{{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{3}}}(p_{2}-p_{3})_{\mu_{1}}+g_{{\mu_{3}}{\mu_{1}}}(p_{3}-p_{1})_{\mu_{2}}],

(34)

S
V_{a,\mu_{1}}
V_{b,\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}eC_{SV_{a}V_{b}}g_{{\mu_{1}}{\mu_{2}}},

(35)

V_{\mu}
S_{a}
S_{b}
p_{1}
p_{2}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}eI^{V}_{\bar{S}_{a}S_{b}}(p_{1}-p_{2})_{\mu},

(36)

S_{a}
S_{b}
S_{c}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}eC_{S_{a}S_{b}S_{c}},

(37)

V_{\mu}
\bar{f}_{k}
f_{l}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}eI^{V}_{f^{\sigma}_{k}f^{\sigma}_{l}}\gamma_{\mu}\omega_{\sigma},

(38)

S
\bar{f}_{k}
f_{l}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}eC^{\sigma}_{S\bar{f}_{k}f_{l}}\omega_{\sigma}.

(39)

In (
38
) and (
39
), we sum over
the right- and left-handed fermion
chiralities
\sigma=\mathrm{R}/\mathrm{L}=\pm
and employ the chirality
projection matrices

\displaystyle\omega_{\pm}=\omega_{\mathrm{R}/\mathrm{L}}=\frac{1\pm\gamma^{5}}{2}.

(40)

As described in Section
2.1
, the various
factors
I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}
correspond to the generators
of the considered gauge theory.
For the
EWSM
, they are provided explicitly in
Appendix
A.3
.
For would-be Goldstone bosons,
C_{G_{a}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}
is
related to
I^{V_{a}}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}
via
WIs
, as
discussed in Sect.
2.6
.
The remaining couplings depend on further details of the theory.
In the
EWSM
, for example, they are given in (
365
)
and (
372
).

In order to unify the above definitions, we generically denote couplings
between three incoming fields
\varphi_{k}
,
\varphi_{l}
, and
\varphi_{m}
by

\mathcal{C}^{(\sigma)}_{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}\varphi_{m}}
,
including a chirality label
\sigma
if fermions are among these fields.
Specifically, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{V_{a}V_{b}V_{c}}

\displaystyle=I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{V}_{b}V_{c}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{SV_{a}V_{b}}

\displaystyle=C_{SV_{a}V_{b}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{V_{c}S_{a}S_{b}}

\displaystyle=I^{V_{c}}_{\bar{S}_{a}S_{b}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{S_{a}S_{b}S_{c}}

\displaystyle=C_{S_{a}S_{b}S_{c}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}_{V_{a}\bar{f}_{k}f_{l}}

\displaystyle=I^{V_{a}}_{f^{\sigma}_{k}f^{\sigma}_{l}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}_{S_{a}\bar{f}_{k}f_{l}}

\displaystyle=C^{\sigma}_{S_{a}\bar{f}_{k}f_{l}}.

(41)

2.5
Ward and Slavnov–Taylor identities

In quantized field theories, the invariance of the action functional under some
field transformation gives rise to relations between
Green functions (GFs)
(see e.g.
Weinberg:1996kr
;
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn
).
For example, field shifts
\varphi_{k}(x)\to\varphi_{k}(x)+\epsilon f_{k}(x)
with infinitesimal
\epsilon
and arbitrary functions
f_{k}(x)

result in the
equations of motion (EOMs)

bohm2001gauge
.
Specifically, for the antighost field,

\displaystyle\Big\langle T\frac{\delta}{\delta\bar{u}^{V_{a}}}\prod_{j}\varphi_{l_{j}}\Big\rangle=\mathrm{i}\Big\langle TsC^{V_{a}}\prod_{j}\varphi_{l_{j}}\Big\rangle,

(42)

where
\langle T\dots\rangle
stands for the vacuum expectation value of the
time-ordered product of fields
bohm2001gauge
.
If the field transformations are a symmetry of the action, we obtain the
well-known
WIs
(see
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn
, and
references therein).
In case of the
BRST
symmetry of a quantized gauge theory, these are represented by
the famous
Slavnov–Taylor (ST)
identities.
Following the discussion in
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn
,
the general form of the
ST
identities reads

\displaystyle s\Big\langle T\prod_{k=1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{k}}\Big\rangle=0.

(43)

An important class of identities is obtained by taking
\varphi_{l_{1}}
as an
antighost field
\bar{u}^{\bar{V}_{a_{1}}}
, and
\varphi_{l_{i}}
as
gauge-fixing functionals
C^{V_{a_{i}}}
for
i=2,\dots,m
.
The remaining fields are chosen as “physical”, i.e. the propagators pointing to the
corresponding field points are
truncated and replaced by on-shell wave functions.
In the notation, these physical fields are
indicated by
\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}
.
Note that they are characterized by

\langle T\dots s\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\dots\rangle=0
,
where the dots stand for any physical fields,
cf.
bohm2001gauge
.
Considering connected
GFs
only,
using both (
15
) and the

EOM
(
42
) for the antighost field,
inserting (
25
),
and finally Fourier transforming to
momentum space, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle T\prod_{i=1}^{m}\Big[p_{i,\mu_{i}}\bar{V}_{a_{i}}^{\mu_{i}}(p_{i})+\xi_{V_{a_{i}}}\eta_{V_{a_{i}}}M_{V_{a_{i}}}\bar{G}_{a_{i}}(p_{i})\Big]\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{c}}=0,

(44)

where the “c” indicates the restriction to the connected part of the GF.
Equation (
44
) represents a very convenient form
of the
ST
identities for reducible, connected
GFs
.
Note that for massless vector-boson fields
V_{a}
, no associated would-be
Goldstone-boson fields
G_{a}
exist, i.e. the corresponding terms with
G_{a}

are absent in this case.

In order to obtain statements about
S
-matrix elements, we
now reformulate these identities in terms of connected, truncated
GFs

G_{\mathrm{c}}^{\underline{\varphi_{l_{1}}},\dots}(p,\dots)
.
Following the conventions of
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn
for

GFs
throughout, we indicate truncated fields by underlining
and take these fields and their momenta as incoming.
By contrast, fields that are not truncated are taken as outgoing
(with incoming momenta).
Since we are only interested in the relations among couplings resulting
from the
ST
identities, it is sufficient to restrict the following
discussion to the lowest
perturbative order, i.e. to tree level.
Due to the choice of
R_{\xi}
gauge-fixing functionals, the lowest-order
propagator
G_{0}^{\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}}
neither mixes vector-boson
fields
V_{a}
with
scalar fields
\phi_{k}
nor would-be Goldstone-boson
fields
G_{a}
with
the remaining scalar fields
H_{m}
.
With (
33
), we obtain the
ST
identity for

truncated
, connected
GFs
in lowest order (indicated by subscript “0”),

\displaystyle\Big\langle T\prod_{i=1}^{m}\Big[p_{i,\mu_{i}}\underline{V_{a_{i}}^{\mu_{i}}}(p_{i})-\eta_{V_{a_{i}}}M_{V_{a_{i}}}\underline{G_{a_{i}}}(p_{i})\Big]\prod_{j=m+1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{c},0}=0.

(45)

Due to its relevance for the subsequent sections, we conclude by
explicitly spelling this identity out for
m=1,2,3
.
For a single, possibly off-shell external vector boson, we obtain

\displaystyle p^{\mu}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu}^{\underline{V^{a}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p,\dots)=\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}G_{0,\mathrm{c}}^{\underline{G^{a}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p,\dots),

(46)

where we have trivially renumbered the
\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{j}}
.
For two vector bosons, (
45
) involves cross terms, leading to

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}p_{2}^{\mu_{2}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{\underline{V^{a_{1}}}\underline{V^{a_{2}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},\dots)+\eta_{V_{a_{1}}}M_{V_{a_{1}}}\eta_{V_{a_{2}}}M_{V_{a_{2}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c}}^{\underline{G^{a_{1}}}\underline{G^{a_{2}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},\dots)

\displaystyle-p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\eta_{V_{a_{2}}}M_{V_{a_{2}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{1}}^{\underline{V^{a_{1}}}\underline{G^{a_{2}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},\dots)-p_{2}^{\mu_{2}}\eta_{V_{a_{1}}}M_{V_{a_{1}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{2}}^{\underline{G^{a_{1}}}\underline{V^{a_{2}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},\dots),

(47)

Finally, for three vector bosons we get

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}p_{2}^{\mu_{2}}p_{3}^{\mu_{3}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}^{\underline{V^{a_{1}}}\underline{V^{a_{2}}}\underline{V^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle-p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}p_{2}^{\mu_{2}}\eta_{V_{a_{3}}}M_{V_{a_{3}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}^{\underline{V^{a_{1}}}\underline{V^{a_{2}}}\underline{G^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle-p_{2}^{\mu_{2}}p_{3}^{\mu_{3}}\eta_{V_{a_{1}}}M_{V_{a_{1}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}^{\underline{G^{a_{1}}}\underline{V^{a_{2}}}\underline{V^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle-p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}p_{3}^{\mu_{3}}\eta_{V_{a_{2}}}M_{V_{a_{2}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}^{\underline{V^{a_{1}}}\underline{G^{a_{2}}}\underline{V^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle+p_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\eta_{V_{a_{2}}}M_{V_{a_{2}}}\eta_{V_{a_{3}}}M_{V_{a_{3}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{1}}^{\underline{V^{a_{1}}}\underline{G^{a_{2}}}\underline{G^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle+p_{2}^{\mu_{2}}\eta_{V_{a_{1}}}M_{V_{a_{1}}}\eta_{V_{a_{3}}}M_{V_{a_{3}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{2}}^{\underline{G^{a_{1}}}\underline{V^{a_{2}}}\underline{G^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle+p_{3}^{\mu_{3}}\eta_{V_{a_{1}}}M_{V_{a_{1}}}\eta_{V_{a_{2}}}M_{V_{a_{2}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{3}}^{\underline{G^{a_{1}}}\underline{G^{a_{2}}}\underline{V^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots)

\displaystyle-\eta_{V_{a_{1}}}M_{V_{a_{1}}}\eta_{V_{a_{2}}}M_{V_{a_{2}}}\eta_{V_{a_{3}}}M_{V_{a_{3}}}G_{0,\mathrm{c},\mu_{2}}^{\underline{G^{a_{1}}}\underline{G^{a_{2}}}\underline{G^{a_{3}}}\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{1}}\dots}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\dots).

(48)

2.6
Generic would-be Goldstone couplings

The lowest-order
WIs
(
46
), (
47
),
and (
48
)
imply identities between couplings
that are obtained by replacing individual vector bosons by would-be
Goldstone bosons.
Here, we explicitly spell out these relations, thereby extending the
notation of generic couplings
from
denner2001oneloop1
;
denner2001oneloop2
.
The corresponding derivations are presented in
Appendix
B
.

VGH
couplings.

Couplings of type
HVV
and
VGH
are connected via

\displaystyle C_{H_{m}V_{a}V_{b}}=-2\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}I^{V_{b}}_{\bar{G}_{a}H_{m}}=-2\eta_{V_{b}}M_{V_{b}}I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{G}_{b}H_{m}},

(49)

which also implies the following identity between
VGH
couplings,

\displaystyle I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{G}_{b}H_{m}}=\frac{\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}}{\eta_{V_{b}}M_{V_{b}}}I^{V_{b}}_{\bar{G}_{a}H_{m}}.

(50)

By virtue of (
23
),
HVV
couplings are related to
vector-boson masses,

\displaystyle C_{H_{m}V_{a}V_{b}}\langle H_{m}\rangle=\frac{2M_{V_{a}}^{2}}{e}\delta_{V_{a}\bar{V}_{b}},

(51)

which involves a sum over the fields
H_{m}
.
In the
EWSM
, this becomes

\displaystyle C_{HV_{a}V_{b}}=\frac{2M_{V_{a}}^{2}}{ev}\delta_{V_{a}\bar{V}_{b}}.

(52)

GVV
couplings.

GVV
couplings are related to pure-gauge
VVV
couplings through

\displaystyle C_{G_{a}V_{b}V_{c}}=I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{V}_{b}V_{c}}\frac{M_{V_{c}}^{2}-M_{V_{b}}^{2}}{\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}}.

(53)

VGG
couplings.

The couplings of type
VGG
can be obtained from the corresponding gauge
group generators.
However, they are also related to
VVV
couplings via

\displaystyle I^{V_{c}}_{\bar{G}_{a}G_{b}}

\displaystyle=I^{V_{c}}_{\bar{V}_{a}V_{b}}\frac{M_{V_{c}}^{2}-M_{V_{a}}^{2}-M_{V_{b}}^{2}}{2\eta_{V_{a}}\eta_{V_{b}}M_{V_{a}}M_{V_{b}}}.

(54)

Through (
53
), this connects
GGH
and

VGG
couplings, as well.

GGH
couplings.

GGH
couplings are obtained from the
VGH
counterparts according to

\displaystyle C_{G_{a}G_{b}H_{m}}=-\frac{M_{H_{m}}^{2}}{\eta_{V_{b}}M_{V_{b}}}I^{V_{b}}_{\bar{G}_{a}H_{m}}=-\frac{M_{H_{m}}^{2}}{\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}}I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{G}_{b}H_{m}},

(55)

which combines with (
49
) into a relation
between
GGH
and
HVV
couplings.
Furthermore, (
23
) implies

\displaystyle C_{G_{a}G_{b}H_{m}}\langle H_{m}\rangle=-\frac{M_{H_{m}}^{2}}{e}\delta_{V_{a}\bar{V}_{b}},

(56)

with
EWSM
form

\displaystyle C_{G_{a}G_{b}H}=-\frac{M_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}}{ev}\delta_{V_{a}\bar{V}_{b}}.

(57)

GHH
couplings.

The relation between
GHH
and
VHH
couplings reads

\displaystyle C_{G_{a}H_{m}H_{n}}=\frac{M_{H_{m}}^{2}-M_{H_{n}}^{2}}{\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}}I^{V_{a}}_{\bar{H}_{m}H_{n}}.

(58)

Both types of couplings vanish in the
EWSM
.

GGG
couplings.

All
GGG
couplings vanish,

\displaystyle C_{G_{a}G_{b}G_{c}}=0.

(59)

G\bar{f}f
couplings.

The couplings of type
G\bar{f}f
and
V\bar{f}f
satisfy the identity

\displaystyle C^{\sigma}_{G_{a}\bar{f}_{k}f_{l}}=\frac{m_{f_{k}}I^{V_{a}}_{f^{\sigma}_{k}f^{\sigma}_{l}}-m_{f_{l}}I^{V_{a}}_{f^{-\sigma}_{k}f^{-\sigma}_{l}}}{\eta_{V_{a}}M_{V_{a}}}.

(60)

HHH
and
H\bar{f}f
couplings.

The remaining couplings of type
HHH
and
H\bar{f}f
depend on the
scalar potential and the Yukawa sector of the theory, respectively.
Thus, they are not related to other couplings via gauge symmetries.
For the example of the
EWSM
, see (
365
)
and (
372
).

Massless limits.

The coupling relations (
50
),
(
53
),
(
54
),
(
55
),
(
58
),
and (
60
)
involve factors that feature the masses of at least one gauge
boson
V_{a}
in their denominator.
Any meaningful way of taking the limit
M_{V_{a}}\to 0
in some gauge
theory should keep couplings well defined.
Consequently, the mass-singular factors should cancel in the massless limit.
This is, however, not directly obvious, as the corresponding
numerators involve combinations of masses of various other fields.
Yet, because the derivation of the above relations is based on

WIs
, this fact is a consequence of the gauge structure of the
theory.

Consider, for example, (
53
) in the

EWSM
, where
V_{a}=A
is the photon field.
3
3

3

In theories that involve massless gauge bosons
V_{a}
from the outset,
the formulation of the above coupling relations is not sensible,
since
V_{a}
is not associated to a would-be Goldstone boson
G_{a}
.
However, in the
WIs
used for the derivation of the
coupling relations, all terms that involve would-be Goldstone bosons
vanish exactly due to the proportionality to
M_{V_{a}}
.
Without ever dividing by
M_{V_{a}}
, we instead end up with equations
stating that the numerators on the r.h.s. of (
50
),
(
53
),
(
54
),
(
55
),
(
58
),
and (
60
)
vanish, and this is what we refer to in this
EWSM
example.
Alternatively, we could implement the
EWSM
as the limit
m_{\gamma}\to 0
of
a more general theory with a massive photon
\gamma
.
Specifically, this requires embedding the
EWSM
into a
larger theory with additional scalars.
These need to decouple from
EWSM
fields in the massless limit.
This means that the l.h.s. of the referred relations must vanish
(i.e. the couplings of the would-be Goldstone boson associated to the
massive photon).
The
EWSM
limit
m_{\gamma}\to 0
is hence taken by replacing the
whole factor on the r.h.s. of these equations by zero.

This relation is only consistent if
I^{A}_{\bar{V}_{b}V_{c}}=0
,
unless
M_{V_{b}}=M_{V_{c}}
.
This is indeed the case:
The only non-vanishing coupling of the photon to other gauge bosons is
that to two W bosons.
Similarly, (
60
) requires the photon to
couple to fermions of the same mass in a chirality-independent
way.
Again, this is the case.

The limit
M_{\mathrm{W}}\to 0
,
M_{\mathrm{Z}}\to 0
from the
EWSM
to the corresponding unbroken theory is taken by
v\to 0
, which can be
realized by
\mu^{2}\to 0
in (
352
).
The would-be Goldstone-boson fields for W and Z bosons become
physical scalar degrees of freedom and replace longitudinal components of
W- and Z-boson fields.
For example, the massless limit of the r.h.s. in (
54
) is finite.
Accordingly, the l.h.s. describes the coupling of these physical
scalars to vector bosons, which are now massless.
On the other hand, the r.h.s. of (
53
)
vanishes because of the quadratic masses in the numerator, implying
zero coupling of a single scalar to two vector bosons.
The limit
v\to 0
does not only cause the masses of all vector boson
fields, but also those of the fermion fields to vanish.
Hence, the r.h.s. of (
60
) remains well
defined for
M_{\mathrm{W}}\to 0
,
M_{\mathrm{Z}}\to 0
despite the
parity-violating nature of the weak interaction.
Again, the limit is finite and results in non-vanishing,
chiral couplings of physical scalars to massless fermions,
for
v=0
.

As we discuss in Sections
4.2
,

4.3
,
and
4.5
, these considerations are important for
the various massless limits of our splitting functions, involving
similar, seemingly mass-singular terms.

2.7
Goldstone-boson equivalence theorem

For vector bosons with mass
M_{V}\neq 0
, there are three independent
polarization vectors.
In any frame of reference, they can be subdivided into two transverse
vectors
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{T},a}
,
a=1,2
, with
\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{T},a}=0

for the 3-dimensional space-like parts
\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{T},a}

and one longitudinal vector

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{L}}
.
We may choose
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{T},a}

as helicity eigenvectors
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\pm}
with eigenvalues
\pm 1
,
while the helicity eigenvalue of
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{L}}
is 0.
The separation into
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{T},a}
and

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{L}}
, however, is frame dependent.
Generally, we can parametrize

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{L}}(p)=\frac{p^{\mu}}{M_{V}}+r^{\mu}(p),

(61)

with a gauge vector
r^{\mu}
that fulfills
r^{2}=0
,
pr=-M_{V}
and
is of
\mathcal{O}\left(M_{V}\right)
for
p^{0}\gg M_{V}
.
It constrains the remaining transverse
polarization vectors via

\displaystyle r\varepsilon_{\mathrm{T},a}=0.

(62)

Specifically, the four-vector
r^{\mu}
defines the frame in which

\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{T},a}
and
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{L}}
are
transverse and longitudinal w.r.t.
p^{\mu}
as three-vectors,
respectively, via the requirement
\boldsymbol{p}\parallel\boldsymbol{r}
.

The
WI
(
45
) makes an important statement about
the high-energy behavior of amplitudes involving external longitudinal
gauge bosons.
The first term in (
61
) grows indefinitely in the
limit where the energy
p^{0}
of the vector boson becomes large w.r.t. its mass
M_{V}
.
This suggests problematic behavior of the associated matrix elements.
However, it turns out that these mass-singular terms cancel
systematically when computing full amplitudes, as a consequence of the

WI
(
45
).
In fact, we have
Chanowitz:1985hj
;
bohm2001gauge
;
Denner:2019vbn

\displaystyle\Big\langle T\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Big[\varepsilon^{\mu_{i}}_{\mathrm{L}}(p_{i})\underline{V^{a_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}}(p_{i})\Big]\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\Big\rangle_{0,\mathrm{c}}=\Big\langle T\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Big[r^{\mu_{i}}_{i}\underline{V^{a_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}}(p_{i})+\eta_{V_{a_{i}}}\underline{G_{a_{i}}}(p_{i})\Big]\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\Big\rangle_{0,\mathrm{c}},

(63)

where the r.h.s. is manifestly well-behaved in the high-energy limit.
In
Chanowitz:1985hj
, this statement is
shown by summing all possible versions of (
45
) that
involve the insertion of
m
gauge-fixing functionals (
1\leq m\leq l
)
and
l-m
additional longitudinal vector bosons
inside
\varphi^{\mathrm{phys}}_{l_{j}}
.
4
4

4

For
l>1
,
this proof is much more involved than the naive approach of simply
applying (
46
) to the contributions proportional
to
p^{\mu_{i}}_{i}/M_{V_{a_{i}}}
, which is invalid due to unphysical
external fields. A less cumbersome, but fundamentally analogous version of this proof is
provided by
Cuomo:2019siu
.

In the following, we use (
63
) for the treatment
of longitudinally polarized vector bosons in the derivation of collinear
factorization formulas, as it allows for the
exact
rearrangement
of terms in the corresponding matrix elements.
For completeness, we note that the tree-level

GBET

Gounaris:1986cr
;
Cornwall:1974km
;
Chanowitz:1985hj
;
Lee:1977eg
;
Vayonakis:1976vz
;
Denner:1996gb

\displaystyle\Big\langle T\prod_{i=1}^{l}\left[\varepsilon^{\mu_{i}}_{\mathrm{L}}(p_{i})\underline{V^{a_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}}(p_{i})\right]\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\Big\rangle_{0,\mathrm{c}}={}\Big\langle T\prod_{i=1}^{l}\left[\eta_{V_{a_{i}}}\underline{G_{a_{i}}}(p_{i})\right]\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{l_{j}}^{\mathrm{phys}}\Big\rangle_{0,\mathrm{c}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\max_{i=1,\dots,l}\left\{\frac{M_{V_{a_{i}}}}{p_{i}^{0}}\right\}\right)

(64)

is obtained as an
approximation
of (
63
),
because all terms involving a factor
r^{\mu_{i}}_{i}
scale
like
\mathcal{O}\big(M_{V_{a_{i}}}/p^{0}_{i}\big)
.

Finally, we comment on the limit
M_{V_{a}}\to 0

of (
63
), for which
\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\mathrm{L}}

is ill-defined due to its mass-singular component.
Naively, this also suggests singular behavior of the corresponding
amplitudes, i.e. the l.h.s. of (
63
).
However, because
r^{\mu}\to 0
, the r.h.s. becomes equal to the
would-be Goldstone-boson amplitude and thereby remains finite;
it even turns into an amplitude for a physical scalar boson, since
the Goldstone boson turns into a physical degree of freedom.
On the other hand, massless vector bosons do not possess longitudinal
polarization states, and there are no associated physical matrix
elements.
Instead, the finiteness of (
64
)
in the massless limit implies the transversality
condition for truncated amplitudes for massless gauge bosons,
i.e. the l.h.s. of (
64
) becomes zero
after replacing the polarization vector

\varepsilon^{\mu_{i}}_{\mathrm{L}}(p_{i})
of the gauge boson
V_{a_{i}}

by its momentum
p_{i}^{\mu_{i}}
if
M_{V_{a_{i}}}\to 0
.

3
Quasi-collinear factorization

3.1
Collinear and quasi-collinear factorization in QED and QCD

We are interested in the behavior of matrix elements for arbitrary
2\to n+1
processes

\displaystyle\varphi_{l_{\mathrm{A}}}(p_{\mathrm{A}})\varphi_{l_{\mathrm{B}}}(p_{\mathrm{B}})\to\varphi_{l_{1}}(p_{1})\dots\varphi_{l_{n+1}}(p_{n+1}),

(65)

in the limit where any two momenta become collinear.
If both of them correspond to particles in the
FS
, we denote the
latter by
i
and
j
.
This situation is called
FS
case in the following.
If one of them lies in the
IS
, while the other one is part of the

FS
, we denote them by
a
and
i
, respectively.
This is referred to as
IS
case.
In the following, we simplify our notation by referring
to
\varphi_{l_{a}}
,
\varphi_{l_{i}}
, and
\varphi_{l_{j}}

as
\varphi_{a}
,
\varphi_{i}
, and
\varphi_{j}
, respectively.
Essentially, this corresponds to absorbing the
multiplet
label

l
into
\varphi
and thereby to the reinterpretation of the latter as

field
.
The matrix elements for the FS and IS cases are written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(n+1)\,\kappa_{i}\kappa_{j}}_{\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}X}\quad\text{(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{acronym}{fs}{{{}}FS} case),}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{M}^{(n+1)\,\kappa_{a}\kappa_{i}}_{\varphi_{a}\varphi_{i}X}\quad\text{(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{acronym}{is}{{{}}IS} case),}

(66)

where all remaining fields are collectively denoted by
X
.
Polarization is denoted by the labels
\kappa
.

It is well known that the matrix elements (
66
) of
massless
QED
and
QCD
exhibit singularities in the collinear limit.
At leading order, in appropriate gauges, these originate from diagram
topologies with an internal line that splits into two external lines,

p_{ij}
\varphi_{ij}
p_{i}
\varphi_{i}
p_{j}
\varphi_{j}

(
FS
case),

\varphi_{a}

p_{a}
p_{i}
\varphi_{i}
p_{ai}
\varphi_{ai}

(
IS
case).

(67)

The intermediate line is denoted by
ij
in the
FS
case and
ai

in the
IS
case.
In these graphs, we have suppressed the remaining
FS
and
IS
particles
X
.
We use white blobs to denote the sum over all possible subdiagrams that
connect the internal line
\varphi_{ij}
or
\varphi_{ai}

with
X
.
For now, we consider ’t Hooft–Feynman gauge, i.e.
R_{\xi}
gauge with
\xi_{V}=1
,
for which spurious propagator poles are absent.
The collinear singularities stem from the denominators of the propagators
associated with
\varphi_{ij}
or
\varphi_{ai}
.
More specifically, evaluating the diagrams
from (
67
) in the collinear limit, one
observes
factorization
of the matrix
elements (
66
) into underlying
n
-particle
matrix elements

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(n)\,\kappa_{ij}}_{\varphi_{ij}X}\quad\text{(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{acronym}{fs}{{{}}FS} case),}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{M}^{(n)\,\kappa_{ai}}_{\varphi_{ai}X}\quad\text{(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{acronym}{is}{{{}}IS} case),}

(68)

with external
\varphi_{ij}
or
\varphi_{ai}
lines, and universal factors.
The latter are singular in the collinear limit and depend on the species
of the collinear particles and their polarization, but not on the
remaining details of the full process (
65
).
Collinear factorization has first been formulated
in
Altarelli:1977zs
for massless
QCD
and is treated by many textbooks, such
as
Ellis1991qcd
;
Peskin:1995ev
;
bohm2001gauge
;
Dissertori:2009
;
Schwartz:2014sze
.

In
QED
and
QCD
with massive fermions, the non-zero masses
regularize the collinear singularities.
The propagators get enhanced, but remain finite, when

p_{ij}^{2}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}\right)
or
p_{ai}^{2}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}\right)
and the
remaining energy scales
Q^{2}
of the
process (
65
) are much larger
than
\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}\right)
.
Here,
m
stands symbolically for any of the participating masses.
In this situation, which is called

quasi-collinear
limit

Catani:2000ef
;
catani2002massivedipole
, factorization
remains
valid
dittmaier1999photonrad
;
dittmaier2008polarized
;
Baier:1973ms
;
Kleiss:1986ct
;
Catani:2000ef
;
catani2002massivedipole
;
Keller:1998tf
.

Collinear singularities and quasi-collinear enhancements are
conveniently investigated in the
Sudakov parametrization
of the
external momenta.
For two
FS
particles
i
and
j
it reads
catani2002massivedipole

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=z\bar{p}^{\mu}+k_{\perp}^{\mu}-\underbrace{\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}+z^{2}m_{ij}^{2}-m_{i}^{2}}{2z(\bar{p}n)}}_{\equiv A_{i}}n^{\mu},\qquad p_{i}^{2}=m_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle p_{j}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(1-z)\bar{p}^{\mu}-k_{\perp}^{\mu}-\underbrace{\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}+(1-z)^{2}m_{ij}^{2}-m_{j}^{2}}{2(1-z)(\bar{p}n)}}_{\equiv A_{j}}n^{\mu},\qquad p_{j}^{2}=m_{j}^{2},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bar{p}k_{\perp}=nk_{\perp}=n^{2},\qquad\bar{p}^{2}=m_{ij}^{2}.

(69)

In this decomposition, the four-vector
\bar{p}^{\mu}
fulfills
\bar{p}^{2}=m_{ij}^{2}
and defines the collinear direction.
The variable
z
determines how the momentum
\bar{p}^{\mu}
is shared by particles
i
and
j
.
The mass-shell conditions
p_{i}^{2}=m_{i}^{2}
and
p_{j}^{2}=m_{j}^{2}
are ensured
via the auxiliary light-like vector
n^{\mu}
.
The space-like vector
k_{\perp}^{\mu}
denotes the transverse
momentum component of the splitting and cancels in the sum
of
p_{i}^{\mu}
and
p_{j}^{\mu}
.
The frame in which its spatial components are perpendicular to those of

\bar{p}^{\mu}
depends
on the precise choice of
n^{\mu}
.
For the
IS
case, we generalize the parametrization
from
catani2002massivedipole
to allow for
a massive incoming particle
a
and a
massive collinear momentum
\bar{p}^{\mu}
.
We get

\displaystyle p_{a}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x}\bar{p}^{\mu}-\underbrace{\frac{m_{ai}^{2}-x^{2}m_{a}^{2}}{2x(\bar{p}n)}}_{\equiv B_{a}}n^{\mu},\qquad p_{a}^{2}=m_{a}^{2},

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-x}{x}\bar{p}^{\mu}+k_{\perp}^{\mu}-\underbrace{\frac{(k_{\perp}^{2}-m_{i}^{2})x^{2}+m_{ai}^{2}(1-x)^{2}}{2x(1-x)(\bar{p}n)}}_{\equiv B_{i}}n^{\mu},\qquad p_{i}^{2}=m_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bar{p}k_{\perp}=nk_{\perp}=n^{2},\qquad\bar{p}^{2}=m_{ai}^{2},

(70)

where the variable
x
determines how the initial momentum
p_{a}
is shared by the
particles
i
and
ai
.
Again, the precise definition of
n^{\mu}
determines how exactly this
limit is taken.
In the fully massless case, the propagator denominators
of
\varphi_{ij}
and
\varphi_{ai}
read

\displaystyle 2p_{i}p_{j}=-\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{z(1-z)},\qquad 2p_{a}p_{j}=-\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{1-x},

(71)

and vanish in the collinear limit
k_{\perp}^{\mu}\to 0
.
In the massive case, we have

\displaystyle p_{ij}^{2}-m_{ij}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{z(1-z)}+\frac{m_{i}^{2}}{z}+\frac{m_{j}^{2}}{1-z}-m_{ij}^{2},

\displaystyle p_{ai}^{2}-m_{ai}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{1-x}+m_{a}^{2}x-\frac{xm_{i}^{2}}{1-x}-m_{ai}^{2}.

(72)

The quasi-collinear enhancement of propagators thus corresponds to the
kinematic regime where
k_{\perp}^{2}\sim\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}\right)
.
More precisely, the quasi-collinear limit may be
defined by
k_{\perp}^{\mu},m\to 0
, while
k_{\perp}^{\mu}/m
is kept fixed.
In practice, this can be realized by uniform rescaling of
k_{\perp}^{\mu}

and all masses
m
with a parameter
\lambda
,

\displaystyle k_{\perp}^{\mu}\to\lambda k_{\perp}^{\mu},\quad

\displaystyle m_{i}\to\lambda m_{i},\quad

\displaystyle m_{j}\to\lambda m_{j},\quad

\displaystyle m_{ij}\to\lambda m_{ij},\quad

\displaystyle\text{(FS case)},

\displaystyle k_{\perp}^{\mu}\to\lambda k_{\perp}^{\mu},\quad

\displaystyle m_{a}\to\lambda m_{a},\quad

\displaystyle m_{i}\to\lambda m_{i},\quad

\displaystyle m_{ai}\to\lambda m_{ai},\quad

\displaystyle\text{(IS case)},

(73)

followed by sending
\lambda\rightarrow 0

Catani:2000ef
;
catani2002massivedipole
.
Thus, quasi-collinear propagators scale with
\lambda^{-2}
.
This scheme reproduces the collinear limit if all masses vanish.
We conventionally write
\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda\right)\equiv\mathcal{O}\left(m\right)
and call
it the
mass order
.

In this framework, the derivation of quasi-collinear factorization
formulas in
QED
and
QCD
proceeds as follows.
First, select and compute the
diagrams (
67
), which we refer to as
type I.
Avoiding gauges that provide further sources of enhanced factors, other
diagrams may be considered as subleading and neglected.
5
5

5

Vector-boson propagators in axial gauges as well as external polarization sums for
massless vector bosons contain terms
\propto\frac{1}{pg}
, where
p
is
the momentum of the vector boson and
g
is some gauge vector.
Depending on the choice of
g
, such contributions can be enhanced in the
quasi-collinear limit. This happens, for example, if
\varphi_{i}
represents
a vector boson, and the momentum
p_{j}
is used as gauge vector for the
polarization vectors of
\varphi_{i}
.

,
6
6

6

Of course, we also require the absence of effects that suppress the
diagram topologies (
67
) by factors
of
\mathcal{O}\left(m\right)
w.r.t. the remaining diagrams contributing
to (
66
).
This will always be assumed in the following.

We call them type II in the following.
Second, evaluate the squared sum of
diagrams of type I in the
parametrizations (
69
)
and (
70
).
Lastly, take (
73
) and keep the terms
of leading
\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda\right)
.

3.2
Factorization in gauge theories with massive vector bosons

The outlined procedure requires more care in theories with massive
vector bosons.
This is due to the mass-singular contributions
\propto 1/M_{V}
in the
longitudinal polarization vectors (
61
) for external
particles
i
,
j
, or
a
.
Specifically, these factors may also render
contributions involving diagrams of type II
formally enhanced in the quasi-collinear limit.
At first sight, this mandates their inclusion, which is
not readily possible in a process-independent manner.
As discussed in Section
2.7
in the context of the

GBET
, the mass-singular factors
must systematically cancel when computing full amplitudes.
These
gauge cancellations
, however, involve
all
contributing diagrams
and cannot be expected to take place within the
subsets of diagrams I and II separately.
Explicitly, in some contributions from the diagrams of
type I, the propagator denominator
that is responsible for the collinear enhancement
may fully cancel against
corresponding factors in the numerator.
7
7

7

This mechanism is illustrated in Appendix
C
.

This yields mass-singular terms without collinear propagator
enhancement.
These terms exhibit the same structure as mass-singular terms from
diagrams of type II, allowing for compensations between the
two types of diagrams.
Simply dropping those of type II may, thus, disturb gauge
cancellations.

In this paper, we devise two different strategies for the solution of
these issues.
Both approaches modify the expressions for the diagrams of
type I in a universal manner, completing gauge cancellations
within this set of diagrams.
Henceforth, dropping all remaining
contributions from diagrams of type II becomes
possible, and the manifest process independence of the factorization
procedure is retained.
In both approaches, the
GBET
and its implications for the cancellation of
mass-singular factors are employed as guiding principle.
In summary, our default strategy rewrites
amplitudes with longitudinally polarized vector bosons
from the outset via (
63
), immediately
replacing the associated mass-singular factors.
We discuss this procedure in Section
4.2.1
.
Our alternative procedure, on the other hand, manually identifies and
performs the gauge cancellations implied by the
GBET
, closely
following the above discussion.
This approach is detailed in Appendix
C
.

In the rest of this section, we describe the remaining details
regarding our derivation of quasi-collinear factorization formulas,
assuming longitudinal polarization vectors are treated properly.
The actual computation of factorization formulas for individual
splitting processes is provided in Sections
4.2
,
4.3
,
and
4.5
, based on the approach outlined here.

We aim to factorize
squared
amplitudes from the
outset, i.e. we start from

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{M}^{(n+1)\,\kappa_{i}\kappa_{j}}_{\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}X}\Big|^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathop{\resizebox{1423121}{0}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\sim$}}}\limits_{\lambda\to 0}}{\textstyle\mathop{\resizebox{1423121}{0}{\hbox{$\textstyle\sim$}}}\limits_{\lambda\to 0}}{\scriptstyle\mathop{\resizebox{1256560}{0}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}\limits_{\lambda\to 0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\resizebox{1212868}{0}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}\limits_{\lambda\to 0}}}\sum_{\varphi_{ij^{\vphantom{\prime}}},\,\varphi_{ij^{\prime}}}\hbox to217.21pt{\vbox to82.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.27165pt\lower-41.47197pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
